Player_White (to Proxy):



The position shows that the White Bishop on d3 is under attack by the Black Pawn on e4. The Bishop is defended by the White Queen on d1, but if Black captures `exd3`, White will recapture `Qxd3`, resulting in a loss of material (White loses a Bishop for a Black Pawn). Therefore, White must move the Bishop to safety or capture the pawn (which is also losing material due to `Nxe4`).

The most logical move is to retreat the Bishop to a safe square where it remains active. The square c4 is a good candidate as it keeps the Bishop on the board, controls the center, and avoids the pawn. Another option is e2, but c4 is more active.

I will play `d3c4`.

Player_White (to Proxy):



The position shows White to move. The Black Knight on d4 is a strong piece, attacking key squares like f3 and e2. However, it is currently under attack by both the White Queen on d1 and the White Bishop on e3.

There are two capturing options:
1.  **Bxd4** (e3d4): White Bishop captures the Knight. Black recaptures with the Queen (**Qxd4**). White's Queen on d1 is then attacked and must move. Material is equal (Bishop for Knight), but Black's Queen is well-placed on d4.
2.  **Qxd4** (d1d4): White Queen captures the Knight.
    *   If Black recaptures with the Queen (**Qxd4**), White can recapture with the Bishop (**Bxd4**).
    *   Sequence: **1. Qxd4 Qxd4 2. Bxd4**.
    *   Material calculation: White loses Queen (9) and gains Knight (3) + Queen (9). Black loses Knight (3) and Queen (9) and gains Queen (9) temporarily.
    *   Net result: White loses Queen, Black loses Knight and Queen. White recaptures Queen with Bishop. Effectively, White trades Queen for Knight + Queen, ending up with a Bishop on d4 and Black having lost a piece (the Knight). White is up a full piece (3 points).
    *   If Black does not recapture, White has simply won a Knight for free.

The move **Qxd4** forces a favorable exchange sequence that wins a piece.

      None check.

      White King.
      - g1h1.
      No check.

      So the only check is Qxf6+.
      But it hangs the Queen.

      So we are left with non-check moves.
      Priorities:
      1. Remove threat of Qxf1#.
      2. Improve position.

      Threat Qxf1# comes from Queen on b5.
      Solution:
      1. Force Queen to move.
         - a2a4 (Pawn attacks).
      2. Block the diagonal with a protected piece?
         - No piece can block c4, d3, e2 safely.

         - UNLESS moving Rook blocks something? No.

           Diagonal b5-f1 passes through e2.
           Rook on e1 is (5,1).
           Diagonal is y = -x + 7.
           e1: 1 = -5 + 7 = 2. No.
           e2: 2 = -5 + 7 = 2. Yes.
           So Rook on e1 does NOT block.
      4. Capture the Queen.
         - No piece attacks b5 except Pawn a4 (after move).
         - Wait, does Queen on d4 attack b5?
           d4 (4,4). b5 (2,5).
           Delta (-2, 1). No.

           e3 (5,3). b5 (2,5).
           Delta (-3, 2). No.
         - Does Rook on f1 attack b5?
           No.
         - Does Rook on a1 attack b5?
           No.
         - So only a4 attacks b5.

      So a2a4 seems the best candidate.

Player_White (to Proxy):



The current position shows that White is under immediate threat of checkmate by the Black Queen on b5. The diagonal b5-f1 is clear (squares c4, d3, e2 are empty), and the White Rook on f1 is pinned to the King on g1 by the geometry of the board (though not strictly pinned in the sense that it can move, moving it away leaves the square f1 accessible). Specifically, if Black plays ...Qxf1+, it would be checkmate because the King on g1 has no escape squares (f2, g2, h2 are blocked by pawns, f1 is occupied by the checking Queen, h1 is covered by the Queen on f1).

White must prevent this mate. The most direct way is to move the Rook on f1. However, moving the Rook to d1, c1, or b1 would leave f1 empty and undefended, allowing Black to play ...Qf1# (moving to the empty square f1 delivers checkmate). The only Rook move that defends the f1 square is moving the Rook to e1. If the Rook is on e1, it attacks f1. If Black then plays ...Qf1+, White can recapture with Rxf1, avoiding mate.

Other options like moving the Queen to block (e.g., Qc4, Qd3) fail because the Black Queen can capture the blocking piece and still maintain the threat on f1. Moving the King (Kh1) also fails because ...Qxf1+ would still be mate (Queen on f1 attacks h1). Attacking the Black Queen with a2a4 is risky because Black can simply play ...Qa6, maintaining the threat on f1, and White would still need to find a defense (like Re1) on the next move.

Therefore, the best and safest move is to retreat the Rook to e1.

make_move f1e1

- **e1e2**: Moves the Rook to e2. Attacks the e4 pawn. Solid, but `e3f4` improves a minor piece which is often higher priority in such structures.

Player_White (to Proxy):



The position shows that White is in check from the Black Queen on a1. The White King on g1 has no safe squares to move to (f1 and h1 are on the attacked rank, and f2, g2, h2 are occupied by White pawns). The only legal moves are to block the check. The available moves are `f4c1` (Bishop blocks on c1) and `e4e1` (Rook blocks on e1).

Both moves block the check along the first rank. However, `e4e1` (Rook to e1) also attacks the Black Queen on a1, creating a tactical threat (although the Queen can likely capture the Rook and deliver checkmate). Between the two, `e4e1` is a more active move challenging the checking piece.
